Chapter 1

INTRODUCTION

This book is a continuation of my previous book (Krouglov, 2006)
| where  | I  tried  | to  | present  | a  coherent  |     | mathematical  |     | description  | of  |
| ------ | --------- | --- | -------- | ------------ | --- | ------------- | --- | ------------ | --- |
economic forces acting on economic markets. In (Krouglov, 2006) I
| also  attempted  |     | to  | mathematically  |     | explain  |     | economic  | processes  |     |
| ---------------- | --- | --- | --------------- | --- | -------- | --- | --------- | ---------- | --- |
underlying an economic growth on markets. I tried to mathematically
| describe     | an  | economic      |     | growth         | in  single-product  |     |     | economy.  | The       |
| ------------ | --- | ------------- | --- | -------------- | ------------------- | --- | --- | --------- | --------- |
| explanation  |     | was  briefly  |     | that  markets  | participants        |     |     | withdraw  | part  of  |
product from markets in the form of savings and use the withdrawn
| product  | in  | consecutive  |     | production  | in  the  | form  | of  | investment.  | The  |
| -------- | --- | ------------ | --- | ----------- | -------- | ----- | --- | ------------ | ---- |
withdrawal drives product’s supply on market down while at the same
time it drives product’s price on markets up, which in turn drives
| product’s  | demand   |     | down.   | When  | an  impact      |     | of  the  | product’s  | price  |
| ---------- | -------- | --- | ------- | ----- | --------------- | --- | -------- | ---------- | ------ |
| increase   | exceeds  | an  | impact  | of    | the  product’s  |     | demand   | decrease   | we     |
observe an economic growth whereas we observe an economic decline
in the opposite situation.
In Chapter 2 of this book I continue examining the phenomenon of
an economic growth and present few mathematical models for this
purpose. The work is based on research done in (Krouglov, 2006)
| where  | the  system  |     | of  ordinary  | differential  |     | equations  |     | was  presented,  |     |
| ------ | ------------ | --- | ------------- | ------------- | --- | ---------- | --- | ---------------- | --- |

2 Alexei Krouglov
which linked economic forces behind the product’s demand, product’s
supply and product’s prices on the market. In this chapter I look into
various effects that savings and investment exert on market of the
single-product economy.
In Chapter 3, I examine an impact of four important economic
concepts on the economic growth – demand, supply, investment, and
debt. I present mathematical models to explore interconnections of
these concepts and study their impacts on an economic growth and
decline. I also build a mathematical model to verify hypothesis that
weak recovery after the financial crisis could be attributed to a decline
of investment that was not compensated by the decrease of interest rate.
In Chapter 4, I examine the phenomenon of economic crises and
present few mathematical models for this purpose. The models for four
economic crises are considered in this chapter. The first model is about
the last financial crisis, which tries to explain how relatively small
disturbances on financial markets have produced large effects in real
economy. I build a model describing how fluctuations on two connected
markets could amplify each other, which is known as resonance
phenomenon. The second model is related to monetary part of the
Japanese economic policy known as Abenomics where a “weird” result
happened. Despite steady purchasing of the bonds by the Bank of Japan
their price decreased and the yield increased. I construct a mathematical
model of the financial market to investigate this phenomenon. The third
model is devoted to the secular stagnation hypothesis, which was
advanced by economist Lawrence Summers. Summers’s ideas were
close to my work where I was developing the model of an economic
growth. I complemented the model with a situation where external
supply of product comes on market. The results of model told the
external supply of product provided with either constant rate or constant
acceleration could cause either a restricted (secular stagnation) or an
unrestricted (secular decline) economic decline. The fourth model is a
simplified model describing four different stages of the Greek economic
crisis (before the Eurozone, before the Euro crisis, after the Euro crisis,

|     |     | Introduction  |     |     | 3   |
| --- | --- | ------------- | --- | --- | --- |
and during the austerity period) and two possible stages of the recovery
(with austere and benign economic transformations). Theoretical result
| is  that  study  | of  different  | stages  | of  the  crisis  | was  performed  | with  |
| ---------------- | -------------- | ------- | ---------------- | --------------- | ----- |
mathematical accuracy.

Chapter 2
SAVINGS AND INVESTMENT
2.1. PREFACE
Chapter 2 examines the phenomenon of an economic growth and
presents several mathematical models for this purpose.
Research is based on the author’s previous work, which was
summarized in (Krouglov, 2006).
In (Krouglov, 2006) the system of ordinary differential equations
was presented, which had linked the economic forces behind the
product’s demand, product’s supply and product’s prices on the market.
The author have aspired to clarity and chosen to understand the
acting economic forces in a single-product economy.
After the author understood the economic forces connecting the
product’s demand, the product’s supply and the product’s prices on
market, he tried to comprehend the phenomenon of an economic growth
that would fit into the said system of ordinary differential equations that
linked the economic forces acting on the market. The author asserts that
an economic growth is conceptually caused since market participants
withdraw partially products from markets in the form of savings and
use the withdrawn products in the form of investment in consecutive

6 Alexei Krouglov
production. That process drives the amounts of products on markets
down and at the same time it drives the products’ prices on markets up
and in turn drives the product’s demand down. If the effect of increase
in the products’ prices exceeds the effect of decrease in the amounts of
products, one can observe the process of economic growth whereas she
can observe the process of economic decline in the opposite situation. A
significant point to note is that products’ savings are used as investment
in order to improve the products’ quality. Thus, an increase of the
products’ prices during periods of economic growth is accompanied by
the continuous improvement of the products’ quality.
In this chapter I look into various effects that savings and
investment exert on a single-product economy.
2.2. MODEL OF ECONOMIC GROWTH
Originally the content of Section 2.2 was described in (Krouglov,
2009).
2.2.1. Introduction
Here in this section I show how process of savings and investment
in a single-product economy changes balance on the market of product,
and creates an economic growth. Concept of the economic growth is
presented through a dual impact of changes of the product’s prices and
changes of the supply-demand equilibrium on market of product.
After supply-demand equilibrium on market of product is reached,
economic growth is achieved through continuous improvement of the
product’s quality. To improve the product’s quality one has to make an
appropriate investment of the product (remember, we are dealing with a
single-product economy). That is done by applying the product’s

|     |     | Savings and Investment  |     |     |     |     |     | 7   |
| --- | --- | ----------------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
savings, i.e., a withdrawal of the appropriate amount of product from
| market.   | That  process       | creates  | a  shortage  |     | of  the  | product  | on        | market,  |
| --------- | ------------------- | -------- | ------------ | --- | -------- | -------- | --------- | -------- |
| violates  | the  supply-demand  |          | equilibrium  | on  | market   | of       | product,  | and      |
drives the product’s price up. In other words, when one pays a bigger
| price  for  | the  improved-quality  |     | product,  |     | she  compensates  |     | (from  | an  |
| ----------- | ---------------------- | --- | --------- | --- | ----------------- | --- | ------ | --- |
economic point of view) for increase of the product’s price caused by
the withdrawal of appropriate amount of product from market in the
process of savings (and consecutive investment) in order to improve the
product’s quality.
On the other hand, a withdrawal of product from market in the form
of savings decreases the available amount of product there. The smaller
| amount  | of  product  | on market is  | replenished  |     | by  | an  increase of  |     | the  |
| ------- | ------------ | ------------- | ------------ | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | ---- |
supply. Thus, withdrawal of the product from market for investment
and replenishment of the product on market by suppliers have opposite
impacts on supply-demand equilibrium on market. As the result, it
could be either a surplus or shortage of product on market at some point
in time but the market forces will try to bring market to new supply-
| demand  | equilibrium in  | the  | long  term.  | Similarly,  |     | withdrawal  |     | of  the  |
| ------- | --------------- | ---- | ------------ | ----------- | --- | ----------- | --- | -------- |
product from market in the form of savings increases the product’s
price in the long term and in turn decreases the product’s demand.
These dual impacts drive a monetary value of the product (equal to the
| product’s  | price  multiplied  |     | by  the  product’s  |     | quantity)  |     | on  market  | in  |
| ---------- | ------------------ | --- | ------------------- | --- | ---------- | --- | ----------- | --- |
opposite directions – a bigger price typically drives the monetary value
up; if the product’s quantity increases it drives the monetary value up,
and if the product’s quantity decreases it drives the monetary value
down. When the monetary value increases in time one can talk about an
economic growth, and when the monetary value decreases in time one
can talk about an economic recession.
In the next section I try to transform verbal description of the
process of an economic growth in single-product economy into the
mathematical description.

| 8   |     |     |     | Alexei Krouglov  |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | --- |
2.2.2. Model of Economy at the Undisturbed State

To turn to mathematical descriptions, when there are no disturbing
| economic forces, the market is in an equilibrium position,  |     |     |     |     |     |     | i.e., the  |
| ----------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | ---------- |
product’s supply and demand are equal, and they are developing with a
constant rate and the product’s price is fixed.
When the balance between the product’s supply and demand is
broken, the market is experiencing economic forces, which act to bring
the market to a new equilibrium position.

Let us calculate now an effect of the product’s savings by
comparing two values of product in money terms taken at the limit
, i.e., when the market comes to a new equilibrium. The first
amount in money terms is equal to , i.e., the product’s
demand after savings multiplied by the product’s price (at
the limit). The second amount in money terms is equal to
, i.e., the product’s demand (if there were no
withdrawal of product from the market)
multiplied by the product’s price (at the limit).

That concludes the second scenario.

2.2.7. Conclusion

Thus, I described several mathematical models of economic growth
in a single-product economy, which show how long-time impacts of
real savings have produced an economic growth in the economy.
| The      | section  | contains        |     | research  |     | of  the  long-term  | impact        | of  real  |
| -------- | -------- | --------------- | --- | --------- | --- | ------------------- | ------------- | --------- |
| savings  | in       | single-product  |     | economy   |     | on  the  dynamics   | of  economic  |           |
growth. Here I presented models describing the cases of economic
growths if savings are done as a one-time withdrawal of the product, a
| constant-rate  |     | continuous  |     | withdrawal  |     | of  the  product,  | a  constant- |     |
| -------------- | --- | ----------- | --- | ----------- | --- | ------------------ | ------------ | --- |
accelerated continuous withdrawal of the product, or an exponential
continuous withdrawal of the product from market.
The models have provided the following conceptual results. In the
case of one-time savings the economy experiences a limited economic
growth in the long run with a minor increase of the product’s price and
a slight increase of the amount of product on market. In the case of
constant-rate continuous savings effect is very similar – the economy
experiences a modest economic growth in the long run with a minor
increase of the product’s price and a stable amount of the product on
market. And only in the case of constant-accelerated continuous savings
the economy can experience a steady economic growth in the long run.
With constant-accelerated continuous savings there is an effect of both
continuous increase of the product’s price and continuous decrease of

| 44           |     |          | Alexei Krouglov  |               |     |              |     |           |
| ------------ | --- | -------- | ---------------- | ------------- | --- | ------------ | --- | --------- |
| the  amount  | of  | product  | on  market.      | It  produces  |     | a  boundary  |     | for  the  |
acceleration rate of internal savings – when rate doesn’t exceed the
| boundary,  | the  | economy  | experiences  | a  continuous  |     | steady  |     | economic  |
| ---------- | ---- | -------- | ------------ | -------------- | --- | ------- | --- | --------- |
growth, and when rate exceeds the boundary, there is a continuous
steady economic decline. The model has shown that with exponential
| continuous  | savings  |     | the  economy  | is  exposed  |     | to  an  | impact  | of  both  |
| ----------- | -------- | --- | ------------- | ------------ | --- | ------- | ------- | --------- |
continuous increase of the product’s price and continuous decrease of
the amount of product on market. However, the mutual combination of
| these  two  | factors  | always  | produces  | a  continuous  |     | steady  |     | economic  |
| ----------- | -------- | ------- | --------- | -------------- | --- | ------- | --- | --------- |
decline.

2.3. GROWTH FACTORS FOR FEW TYPES
OF INVESTMENTS

Originally the content of Section 2.3 was compiled in 2011 but was
not published.

2.3.1. Introduction

| This  | section  | is a  | continuation  | of  the previous work  |     |     |     | (Krouglov,  |
| ----- | -------- | ----- | ------------- | ---------------------- | --- | --- | --- | ----------- |
2006; 2009) where it was presented a mathematical model describing
economic forces acting on economic markets through the system of
ordinary differential equations. Particularly, in (Krouglov, 2006; 2009)
it was built a dynamic model explaining the impact of economic forces
| on  economic  |           | growth  | in  market    | economy.  | The  | cause  | of        | economic  |
| ------------- | --------- | ------- | ------------- | --------- | ---- | ------ | --------- | --------- |
| growth        | is  that  | market  | participants  | withdraw  |      | some   | products  | from      |
markets in the form of savings and use the withdrawn products in
| consecutive  | production  |     | in  the  | form  of  | investments.  |     | That  | procedure  |
| ------------ | ----------- | --- | -------- | --------- | ------------- | --- | ----- | ---------- |
drives the products’ prices on market up and at the same time it drives
the amounts of products on market down. When effect of increase of

Savings and Investment 45
the products’ prices exceeds effect of decrease of the amounts of
products one can observe the phenomenon of an economic growth
whereas she can observe the phenomenon of an economic decline in the
opposite situation. An important point is that the products’ savings are
used as consecutive investment in order to improve the products’
quality. Thus, an increase of the products’ prices during the periods of
economic growth is accompanied by a continuous improvement of the
products’ quality.
In the current section I look into various effects that savings and
investment exert on a single-product economy.
2.3.2. Economic Growth Induced by Investments
Here in this section I present how process of savings in a single-
product economy changes the situation on market of product, and
creates an economic growth.
Concept of the economic growth is presented as a dual impact of
changes in product’s prices and changes in supply-demand equilibrium
on the market of product.
After supply-demand equilibrium on the market of product is
reached, the economic growth is essentially achieved through
continuous improvement in the product’s quality. To improve the
product’s quality one has to make an appropriate investment of product
(remember, we are dealing with a single-product economy here). That
is done by applying the product’s savings, i.e., withdrawing an
appropriate amount of product from the market. That process creates a
temporal or permanent shortage of product on the market, which
violates supply-demand equilibrium for the product on market, and
drives the product’s price up. In other words, a higher price for the
improved-quality product compensates (from a model’s point of view)
for an increase of the product’s price caused by partial withdrawal of

| 46  |     | Alexei Krouglov  |     |     |
| --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- |
the product’s quantity from market via the product’s savings (and its
consecutive investment) in order to improve the product’s quality.
On the other hand, withdrawal of the product from market in the
form of savings decreases available amount of product. This reduced
amount of product on market is compensated by a rise of the product’
supply. Thus, withdrawal of product from market for investment and
| replacement  | of  product  | on  market  | by  the  suppliers  | have  opposite  |
| ------------ | ------------ | ----------- | ------------------- | --------------- |
impacts with regard to the supply-demand equilibrium on market. As a
result, it may demonstrate itself either as a surplus or shortage of the
product on market at some point in time but market forces will act to
bring market back to new supply-demand equilibrium in the long term.
Similarly, withdrawal of the product from market in the form of savings
increases the product’s price in the long term. These dual impacts drive
the monetary amount (or value) of product (equal to the product’s price
| multiplied  | by  the  product’s  | quantity)  | on  market  | in  the  opposite  |
| ----------- | ------------------- | ---------- | ----------- | ------------------ |
directions – a higher price typically drives the monetary value up while
the monetary value can go either up (if the product’s quantity increases)
| or  go  down  | (if  the  | product’s  quantity  | decreases).  | Correspondingly,  |
| ------------- | --------- | -------------------- | ------------ | ----------------- |
increase of the monetary value corresponds to an economic growth, and
decrease of the monetary value corresponds to an economic recession.
Let us turn to a mathematical description now. When there are no
disturbing economic forces, market is in equilibrium position, i.e., the
product’s supply and demand are equal, and they are developing with a
constant rate and the product’s price is unchanged.
When balance between the product’s supply and demand is broken,
market starts experiencing economic forces, which act to bring the
market to a new equilibrium position.
These economic forces are described by the following ordinary

Savings and Investment 51
The effect of the product’s savings can be viewed by comparing
two values of product amounts in money terms taken at the limit
, i.e., when market of product comes to a new equilibrium. The
first value is 1, i.e., the product of the product’s
demand (after savings) multiplied by the product’s price at
the limit. The second value is , i.e., the product of the
product’s demand (as if there were no savings)
multiplied by the product’s price at the limit.

2.3.2.2. Growth for Constant-Accelerated Investments
That scenario is represented by a constant-accelerated continuous
withdrawal of the product’s savings from market. At some point in time
the equilibrium situation was broken, and the amount of
product’s savings increases since time according to
following formula,
(2.3.38)

Thus, all cases to obtain solutions of differential equations from the

Therefore, at the limit for the withdrawal of product from
market causes a boundless increase of the product’s price. Here at the
limit for the withdrawal of product from market either
increases or decreases the product’s demand depending on an
acceleration rate. The withdrawal of savings with a moderate
acceleration rate increases the product demand on market in the long
run. The withdrawal of savings with a large acceleration rate decreases
the product demand on market in the long run. Thus, the withdrawal of
savings with a moderate acceleration rate raises the value of
product in money terms on market in the long run, and withdrawal of
savings with a large acceleration rate decreases the product demand on
market in the long run,

2.3.3. Maximal Growth Rates Induced by Investments
In previous section I have shown how savings create an economic
growth. Two main cases there are constant-rate savings (roughly
corresponding to the developed economies situation) and continuous-
acceleration savings (roughly corresponding to the developing
economies situation).
In this part I show how the process of savings has dual impacts on
the total income generated in economy and on the overall investments

| I  define       | instantaneous  |       |            | net  revenue   | generated  |                | in        | economy  | as      |

| multiplication  | of             | the   | product’s  | price          | by  the    | instantaneous  |           | rate     | of      |

the value added) by the instantaneous rate of product’s supply less the

additional income to the monetary value of product’s investment as
instantaneous aggregate return on investment in economy. I prefer to
work with instantaneous values in order to remove time periods related
to the revenue, income, and rate of return on investment.

| 72  |     |     | Alexei Krouglov  |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
considered – constant-rate savings and constant-acceleration savings.
| For  constant-rate  |     | savings,  |     | the  withdrawal  |     | of  | product  from  | market  |
| ------------------- | --- | --------- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | -------------- | ------- |
causes an increase of the product’s price and practically doesn’t change
the product’s demand. That increases the value of product in money
terms on market in the long run. For constant-acceleration savings, the
withdrawal of product from market causes a boundless increase of the
product’s price. Also the withdrawal of product from market either
| increases  | or  | decreases  | the  | product’s  |     | demand  | depending  | on  an  |
| ---------- | --- | ---------- | ---- | ---------- | --- | ------- | ---------- | ------- |
acceleration rate. The withdrawal of savings with moderate acceleration
rate increases the demand on market in the long run. The withdrawal of
savings with large acceleration rate decreases the demand on market in
| the  long  | run.  | Thus,  | the  | withdrawal  | of  | savings  | with  a  | moderate  |
| ---------- | ----- | ------ | ---- | ----------- | --- | -------- | -------- | --------- |
acceleration rate increases the value of product in money terms on
| market,  | and  withdrawal  |     | of  | savings  | with  | a  large  | acceleration  | rate  |
| -------- | ---------------- | --- | --- | -------- | ----- | --------- | ------------- | ----- |
decreases the value of product in money terms on market.
In this section it was explored how the process of savings has dual
| impact  | on  both  | the  | income  | generated  |     | in  economy  | and  | on  the  |
| ------- | --------- | ---- | ------- | ---------- | --- | ------------ | ---- | -------- |
investments. By taking a ratio of these two quantities the return on
investments was inspected for single-product economy.
| Instantaneous  |     | revenue  |     | was  | defined  | as  multiplication  |     | of  the  |
| -------------- | --- | -------- | --- | ---- | -------- | ------------------- | --- | -------- |
product’s price by the instantaneous rate of product’s supply less the
instantaneous rate of savings. Instantaneous income was defined as
multiplication of the change of product’s price by the instantaneous rate
of product’s supply less the instantaneous rate of savings. The ratio of
instantaneous income to the monetary value of product’s investment
was defined as instantaneous return on investment.
| For  | the  scenario  |     | of  a  | constant-rate  |     | withdrawal of  | savings  | from  |
| ---- | -------------- | --- | ------ | -------------- | --- | -------------- | -------- | ----- |
market, the value of instantaneous revenue is fixed, and the value of
| instantaneous  |     | income  | equals  |     | to  zero.  | Correspondingly,  |     | the  |
| -------------- | --- | ------- | ------- | --- | ---------- | ----------------- | --- | ---- |
instantaneous return on investment as a ratio of instantaneous income to
value of the product’s investment in money terms equals to zero too.
| Thus  | for  the  | scenario  |     | of  a  constant-acceleration  |     |     | withdrawal  | of  |
| ----- | --------- | --------- | --- | ----------------------------- | --- | --- | ----------- | --- |
savings from market, the value of instantaneous revenue increases, and

|             | Savings and Investment  |         |                            | 73   |
| ----------- | ----------------------- | ------- | -------------------------- | ---- |
| the  value  | of  instantaneous       | income  | is  fixed.  Respectively,  | the  |
instantaneous return on investment as a ratio of instantaneous income to
value of the product’s investment in money terms is positive but it
asymptotically approaches zero.

Chapter 3
ECONOMIC TRENDS
3.1. PREFACE
Chapter 3 continues examining the phenomenon of an economic
growth and contains a few more mathematical models for this purpose.
The author continues to explore the economic processes in a single-
product economy.
The author researches four important economic concepts related to
an economic growth – demand, supply, investment, and debt. He
presents mathematical models to examine the interconnections of these
four concepts, and explores their impacts on an economic growth and
decline in money terms.
The author has also conceived hypothesis that a weak recovery after
the financial crisis can be attributed to a decline of the investment that
is not entirely compensated by the decrease of an interest rate. He tried
to build a simplified mathematical model dealing with aforesaid
economic quantities to either confirm or refute the hypothesis.

76 Alexei Krouglov
3.2. MODEL OF CREDIT EXPANSION
Originally the content of Section 3.2 was described in (Krouglov,
2015a).
3.2.1. Introduction
Economic concepts of credit and debt are very important in modern
economy. Here I present a model, which I intend to use later as a tool to
explore the impact of these phenomena on economy.
Below I use a mathematical model of the market of single-product
economy. Economic forces acting on market represent inherent
economic forces of demand and supply complemented with the forces
caused by both credit expansion and corresponding debt servicing. The
actions of market forces are expressed through the system of ordinary
differential equations.
The model continues the previous work on an economic growth and
decline in money terms done in (Krouglov, 2014b).
Though the presented model carries mostly theoretical character, it
will be later expanded to deal with more sophisticated economic
phenomena.
3.2.2. Model of Economy at the Undisturbed State
Work presented in this section is based on the framework of
mathematical dynamics of economic systems developed in (Krouglov,
2006; 2009).
When there are no disturbing economic forces, market is in
equilibrium position, i.e., the supply of and demand for product are

Economic Trends  77
equal, they are developing with a constant rate and a price of the
product is fixed.
I assume the market stayed in an equilibrium position until time

84 Alexei Krouglov
3.2.4. Economic Implications
A single-product economic model of the credit expansion presented
here can be briefly described as following. At first, the demand for
product and supply of it were equal, and the market was undisturbed.
Then the demand for product in economy was increased by
assuming a constant-rate growing debt. The credit expansion in turn
caused an increase of the debt servicing cost. Since the debt servicing
cost was proportional to the accumulated amount of debt, i.e., an
integral of the assumed debt over the time, the amount of debt servicing
cost was growing with an accelerated rate and eventually exceeded the
amount of accumulated debt. Afterwards, the credit expansion was
changed into a credit contraction in the sense that the demand for
product was reduced by the superposition of the amounts of debt and
debt servicing cost since that moment.
During the credit expansion period of a limited length the amended
demand exceeded the supply and that created the product’s shortage on
market, which interrupted supply-demand equilibrium and drove the
product’s price up. On the other hand, an increase of the product’s price
decreased the product’s demand. As a general rule, the dual impact of
the price’s increase and demand’s decrease caused by the debt’s
increase with constant rate induces a restricted short-term economic
growth in money terms (see Krouglov, 2014b). However, depending on
the model characteristics, the said dual impact could cause either an
economic growth in money terms or economic decline in money terms
of finite value (e.g., short-time fluctuations could distort a growth
trend).
During the credit contraction period, which had an unlimited extent,
the amended demand went below the supply and it created the product’s
surplus on market, which interrupted supply-demand equilibrium and
drove the product’s price down. On the other hand, a decrease of the
product’s price increased the product’s demand. The dual impact of the

Economic Trends 85
price’s decrease and demand’s increase could theoretically cause either
an economic growth in money terms or an economic decline in money
terms. Though, the amount of debt servicing cost in the model grew
with an accelerated rate, which created an effect of the unlimited long-
term economic decline in money terms.
It is important to note the debt structure used in the model is a “roll
over” debt, which excludes the principal’s repayment. It allows working
with the converged values in model, and discounting distortions caused
by the minor short-time effects.
The model presented here is rather a model tool, which is used to
explore an economic impact caused to markets by the accumulated
debt. The model examined a scenario when the amount of debt was
growing with constant rate (i.e., as a linear function), which caused the
amount of debt servicing cost to grow with constant acceleration (i.e.,
as a quadratic function). In these circumstances, the amount of debt
servicing cost exceeded the amount of debt at some point in time.
Afterwards, a credit expansion would be changed into a credit
contraction and would induce an unlimited long-term economic decline
in money terms. The model can be expanded further if one wants to
explore more complex and practical scenarios.
3.2.5. Conclusion
Presented here is a mathematical model that investigates economic
effects caused by the credit expansion in single-product economy.
Initially, the demand for product was increased by the assumption of a
constant-rate growing debt. The accumulating debt caused an increase
of the debt servicing cost, where the amount of accumulated cost
eventually exceeded the amount of accumulated debt. Accordingly, the
credit expansion was changed into the credit contraction afterwards in
the sense that the amended demand for product was reduced by the debt
servicing cost.

86 Alexei Krouglov
The model uses a “roll over” debt, which allows ignoring
distortions caused by the minor short-time effects.
The author uses model as a tool to explore an economic impact of
the debt on markets. Scenario was examined when the amount of debt
was growing with a constant rate and the amount of debt servicing cost
was growing with an accelerated rate, where the latter amount in time
exceeded the former amount. Afterwards, a credit expansion would be
changed into a credit contraction and would produce an unlimited long-
term economic decline in money terms. The model can be expanded
further to explore more complex and practical economic phenomena.
3.3. ECONOMIC GROWTH AND DEBT
Originally the content of Section 3.3 was described in (Krouglov,
2015b).
3.3.1. Introduction
There are four important economic concepts in modern economy:
demand, supply, investment, and debt. Here I present a mathematical
model, where I examine the interconnections of these four concepts and
explore their impacts on an economic growth and decline in money
terms.
Below I describe a mathematical model of market of single-product
economy. Economic forces acting on the market represent inherent
market forces of demand and supply complemented with economic
forces caused by the investment, debt and corresponding debt servicing.
The actions of market forces are expressed through the system of
ordinary differential equations.

decline in money terms done in (Krouglov, 2014b) and previous work
on credit expansion and contraction done in (Krouglov, 2015a).
Though the presented model has mostly theoretical character, it can
be expanded to deal with more sophisticated economic phenomena.

3.3.2. Model of Economy at the Undisturbed State

Concepts and methodology presented in this section are based on
the mathematical dynamics framework of economic systems developed
in (Krouglov, 2006; 2009).
When there are no disturbing economic forces, the market is in
equilibrium position, i.e., the supply of and demand for product are
equal, they are developing with a constant rate and a price of the
product is fixed.

3.3.4. Model of Constant-Rate Debt and Constant-
Acceleration Investment

Here I present a model of single-product economy where the credit
is increasing with a constant rate and the investment is increasing with a
constant acceleration.

3.3.5. Economic Implications
A single-product economical model of the credit expansion
presented here can be briefly described as following. Initially, when the
demand for product and supply of it are equal, the market is
undisturbed.
The first scenario in model is following. The demand for product on
market is increased by assuming a constant-rate growing debt.
Simultaneously, the supply of product on market was decreased by a

Economic Trends 105
constant-rate growing investment. The credit expansion in turn causes
an increase of the debt servicing cost. Since the debt servicing cost is
proportional to accumulated amount of debt, i.e., an integral of the
assumed debt over the time, the amount of debt servicing cost is
growing with a constant acceleration.
Thus, the sum of both accumulated amount of investment and
accumulated amount of debt grows with a constant rate, and increases
an excess of the demand over supply. On the contrary, the accumulated
amount of debt servicing cost grows with a constant acceleration, and
increases an excess of the supply over demand. Eventually, the
accumulated amount of debt servicing cost exceeds the sum of
accumulated amount of investment and accumulated amount of debt.
Hence, it initially takes place a limited short-term economic growth in
money terms and afterwards it takes place an unrestricted long-term
economic decline in money terms.
The second scenario in model is following. The demand for product
on market is increased by assuming a constant-rate growing debt.
Simultaneously, the supply of product on market was decreased by a
constant-acceleration growing investment. The credit expansion in turn
causes an increase of the debt servicing cost. Since the debt servicing
cost is proportional to accumulated amount of debt, i.e., an integral of
the assumed debt over the time, the amount of debt servicing cost grows
with a constant acceleration.
Thus, the sum of accumulated amount of investment and
accumulated amount of debt grows with a constant acceleration, and
increases an excess of the demand over supply. Likewise, the
accumulated amount of debt servicing cost grows with a constant
acceleration and increases an excess of the supply over demand. Here,
eventual outcome depends on the quantitative values of two
accelerations.
If the acceleration rate of debt servicing cost exceeds acceleration
rate of investment, the temporal axis is broken into two intervals.
Initially, it takes place a limited short-term economic growth in money

| 106  |     |     | Alexei Krouglov  |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| ---- | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
terms. Afterwards, it takes place an unrestricted long-term economic
decline in money terms.
If the acceleration rate of investment exceeds acceleration rate of
| debt  servicing  | cost,       | the      | same  | process  | takes  | place       | over     | the  | whole  |
| ---------------- | ----------- | -------- | ----- | -------- | ------ | ----------- | -------- | ---- | ------ |
| temporal         | axis,  and  | depends  |       | on       | the    | difference  | between  |      | two    |
accelerations. If the acceleration rate of investment slightly exceeds
acceleration rate of debt servicing cost, it takes place an unrestricted
long-term economic growth in money terms. On the other hand, if the
acceleration rate of investment considerably exceeds acceleration rate
of debt servicing cost, it takes place an unrestricted long-term economic
decline in money terms.

3.3.6. Conclusion

Presented here is a simplified mathematical model of single-product
economy that investigates how economic concepts of investment, debt,
demand and supply interact and affect an economic growth and decline

Economic Trends 107
scenario delivers an unrestricted long-term economic decline in money

Though the presented model has mostly theoretical character, it can
be expanded further to deal with more sophisticated economic
phenomena. It is already advanced to be a simple economic tool but it is
not yet fully developed to solve practical economic tasks.
3.4. MODEL OF ECONOMIC TREND
Originally the content of Section 3.4 was described in (Krouglov,
2016).
3.4.1. Introduction
Good understanding of an economic growth is important in
economics. Additionally, significance was emphasized by the weak
economic recovery after the Great Recession. Another importance is
contributed by the desire to alleviate future financial crises.
Let us first discuss the phenomenon of an economic growth. Here is
how I view it following the framework in (Krouglov, 2006; 2009).
There is base supply-demand field formed on three major economic
quantities: supply, demand, and price. At equilibrium the quantities of
supply and demand are equal. When an equality of the supply and
demand is broken then third quantity, the price, is amended in order to
bring the base field back at equilibrium by affecting quantities of both
the supply and demand.
Here comes an economic growth. Imagine the situation where a
fractional quantity of the supply is removed from market. (The removed
quantity of supply is intended for investment.) How would the base
supply-demand field react to the situation? – Equality of the supply and

108 Alexei Krouglov
demand will be broken, the price will be increasing and the demand will
tend to shrink. How would the demand in money terms react? (The
demand in money terms is the product of a quantity of demand
multiplied by a price.) – If price increases faster than demand shrinks,
then the demand in money terms increases. If price increases slower
than demand shrinks, then the demand in money terms decreases. The
increment of demand in money terms is associated with an economic
growth while the decrease of demand in money terms is associated with
an economic decline.
Thus, general explanation of an economic growth is presented as
economic phenomenon caused by the partial use of supply for
investment’s purposes. Furthermore, few particular applications of an
economic growth were examined in (Krouglov, 2014a; 2014b).
The above framework was expanded in (Krouglov, 2015a; 2015b)
by inclusion of the debt maintenance and debt accumulation. A joint
employment of the concepts of investment, debt maintenance and debt
accumulation allowed modeling of economic recessions. In particular,
mutual application of these concepts was used in an attempt of
modeling of the Greek crises (Krouglov, 2015c).
The subsequent interpretation of the foresaid concepts prompted a
following hypothesis for the amendment of economic growth. Decline
of the investment causes a decrement of economic growth (i.e., the
economic trend flattens). To counteract process of the economic
deterioration the monetary authorities decrease an interest rate as the
compensatory measure. Decline of the interest rate decreases cost of the
debt maintenance and thereafter increases an economic growth (i.e., the
economic trend steepens). If the effect of economic trend’s steepening
prevails over effect of economic trend’s flattening, then increase of an
economic growth takes place (i.e., the entire economic trend steepens).
On the other hand, if the effect of economic trend’s flattening prevails
over effect of economic trend’s steepening, then decrease of an
economic growth takes place (i.e., the entire economic trend flattens).

| --- | --- | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | ---- |
The aim was to build a mathematical model in order to either verify
or reject the stated above hypothesis.
We have viewed an entire economic process as situated in the
| domain         | of  real  | economy.      |         | It         | looks    | like  this  | domain    | is  enough  | for       |
| -------------- | --------- | ------------- | ------- | ---------- | -------- | ----------- | --------- | ----------- | --------- |
| understanding  |           | of  economic  |         | processes  |          | related     | to  both  | an          | economic  |
| growth         | and       | economic      | trend.  |            | As  can  | be          | seen  in  | (Krouglov,  | 2013),    |
inclusion of the monetary market exaggerates real economic outcome.
In any case, the effects of monetary market are not considered here.
| I  present  |     | further  | mathematical  |     |     | models  | that  try  | to  support  | the  |
| ----------- | --- | -------- | ------------- | --- | --- | ------- | ---------- | ------------ | ---- |
expressed above argumentations and reasoning.
Another aim was to compare theoretical results of amendment of an
economic growth with economic trends observable in practice after the
financial crisis. For this purpose, the graphical drawings to illustrate
amendment of an economic trend are presented.

3.4.2. Outline of the Mathematical Apparatus

I deploy a mathematical model of the market for single-product
economy, which can give us an explicit observation of interactions
among different economic variables. Economic forces acting on the

accumulation and corresponding debt servicing. The market actions are
expressed through the system of ordinary differential equations.

equilibrium position, i.e., the supply of and demand for product are

Presented in this section is a single-product economical model that
describes an economic growth through the interactions between
investment and credit expansion as following. Initially, the demand for
product and supply of it are equal and the market is undisturbed.
The next scenario considered in the model is following. The
demand for product on market is increased by undertaking debt that
grows with a constant rate. Simultaneously, the supply of product was
decreased and partially removed from the market through investment
that grows with a constant acceleration. The credit expansion in turn
causes increase of the debt servicing cost. Since the debt servicing cost

| 120  |     |     | Alexei Krouglov  |     |     |     |     |
| ---- | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
is proportional to accumulated amount of debt, i.e., an integral over
time of the assumed debt, the amount of debt servicing cost is growing
with a constant acceleration.
| Thus,  | the  | sum  | of  accumulated  | amount  | of  investment  |     | and  |
| ------ | ---- | ---- | ---------------- | ------- | --------------- | --- | ---- |
accumulated amount of debt grows with a constant acceleration and it
increases the excess of demand over supply. Likewise, the accumulated
amount of debt servicing cost grows with a constant acceleration and it
increases the excess of supply over demand. Here, the outcome depends
on relations between the quantitative values of two accelerations.
| If  | the  acceleration  |     | rate  of  | debt  servicing  | cost  | exceeds  | the  |
| --- | ------------------ | --- | --------- | ---------------- | ----- | -------- | ---- |
acceleration rate of investment then a temporal axis is divided in two
intervals. Originally, there is a limited short-term economic growth in
money terms. Later, there is an unrestricted long-term economic decline
in money terms.
If the acceleration rate of investment exceeds the acceleration rate
of debt servicing cost then an identical process takes place over the
| entire  temporal  |     | axis  | that  depends  | on  the  difference  |     | between  | two  |
| ----------------- | --- | ----- | -------------- | -------------------- | --- | -------- | ---- |
accelerations. If the acceleration rate of investment slightly exceeds the
acceleration rate of debt servicing cost then there is an unrestricted
long-term economic growth in money terms. On the other hand, if the
acceleration rate of investment considerably exceeds the acceleration
| rate  of  | debt  | servicing  | cost  then  | there  is  an  | unrestricted  | long-term  |     |
| --------- | ----- | ---------- | ----------- | -------------- | ------------- | ---------- | --- |
economic decline in money terms.

At first glance, the transformation of economic trends presented
here agrees with tendencies that show a weak recovery after the latest
financial crisis and are both observable in practice and described in
empirical economic literature.

3.4.5. Conclusion

| Presented  | here is  | a  simplified  | mathematical  | model  that tries  | to  |
| ---------- | -------- | -------------- | ------------- | ------------------ | --- |
explain an observable weak recovery after the financial  crisis. The
author has built the model to either confirm or refute the hypothesis that

Economic Trends 133
the weak recovery can be attributed to a decline of the investment that
is not entirely compensated by the decrease of an interest rate.
The presented model has confirmed the hypothesis.
The author has described economic process that showed how a
transformation of the economic trends occurs. He has shown a graphical
representation of the transformation and distinguished the cases when
transformation of the economic trend occurred with or without
fluctuations of short-time variations. Graphical representation of the
transformation has nearly agreed with practically observable tendencies
that corresponded to the recovery processes after the financial crisis.

Chapter 4
MODELS OF THE CRISES
4.1. PREFACE
Chapter 4 examines the phenomenon of economic crises and
contains few mathematical models developed for this purpose.
Research is based on the framework developed in (Krouglov, 2006).
In (Krouglov, 2006) the system of ordinary differential equations was
advanced, which had linked economic forces behind the product’s
demand, product’s supply and product’s prices on market.
That work was continued with examining the phenomenon of an
economic growth, which was generated since market participants
withdraw partially products from markets in the form of savings and
use the withdrawn products in the form of investment in production.
The author built several mathematical models for this purpose.
The work was supplemented with a study of four important
economic concepts related to the growth – demand, supply, investment,
and debt. The author built models to examine the interconnections of
these four concepts and explore their impacts on an economic growth
and decline in money terms.

| 136  |     |     |     | Alexei Krouglov  |     |     |     |     |     |
| ---- | --- | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
The aforementioned economic processes were explored in a single-
product economy. The models of four economic crises were considered
in this chapter.
| The  | first  | model  | described  |     | the  recent  | financial  |     | crisis,  | which  |
| ---- | ------ | ------ | ---------- | --- | ------------ | ---------- | --- | -------- | ------ |
produced a theoretical challenge of explaining how relatively small
disturbances on financial markets had produced large consequences in
real economy. The author built a mathematical model describing the
interaction of economic forces acting on connected economic markets –
financial market and real-product market. He showed how fluctuations
on two connected markets could amplify each other, which was known
as resonance phenomenon.
The second model was related to a monetary part of the Japanese
economic policy known as Abenomics. The Bank of Japan was buying
Japanese government bonds. Since demand for the bonds increased, the
| price  of  | them       | should  | increase  |          | and  correspondingly  |     | the        | bond     | yield  |
| ---------- | ---------- | ------- | --------- | -------- | --------------------- | --- | ---------- | -------- | ------ |
| should     | decrease.  |         | However,  | “weird”  | outcome               |     | happened.  | Despite  |        |
purchasing of the bonds by the Bank of Japan their price decreased and
| the  yield  | increased.  |     | The          | author  | built  mathematical  |     |           | model  | of  the  |
| ----------- | ----------- | --- | ------------ | ------- | -------------------- | --- | --------- | ------ | -------- |
| financial   | market      | to  | investigate  |         | this  phenomenon.    |     | Economic  |        | forces   |
acting on market represented both the inherent demand and supply
forces and government interventions and were expressed through the
system of ordinary differential equations. The model was presented in
two steps. At the first step, the author simplified picture to show the
| underlying  | impact  |     | of  fluctuations.  |     | At  the  | second  | step,  | the  | author  |
| ----------- | ------- | --- | ------------------ | --- | -------- | ------- | ------ | ---- | ------- |
introduced back amortization force, which defined the inflection point
in economic system.
The model is a second result, which the author has learned from
lessons of the Great Recession.
The third model was devoted to the so-called secular stagnation
hypothesis, which with respect to today’s economical conditions was
| proposed  | by  | economist  | Lawrence  |     | Summers.  | Lawrence  |     | Summers’s  |     |
| --------- | --- | ---------- | --------- | --- | --------- | --------- | --- | ---------- | --- |
ideas were close to the author’s work on conjectural economic situation,
which he had been analyzing using the model of an economic growth

Models of the Crises 137
(Krouglov, 2006; 2009). The author used mathematical model of an
economic growth based on ordinary differential equations describing
the demand and supply forces acting on market in hypothetical single-
product economy. The author complemented the model with a situation
where the external supply of product came on market. He showed the
external supply of product provided with either constant rate or constant
acceleration could cause either a restricted (secular stagnation) or an
unrestricted (secular decline) economic decline.
The fourth model was a simplified economic model describing four
stages of the current Greek economic crisis (before the Eurozone,
before the Euro crisis, after the Euro crisis, and during the austerity
period) and two possible stages of the recovery (possible austere
economic transformation and possible benign economic
transformation). Theoretical result was that the study of different stages
of the crisis was performed with mathematical accuracy. As a practical
result, it was objectively shown that an investment in benign conditions
of the sustained demand would produce a bigger economic growth in
money terms than the investment in austere conditions of the reduced
demand.
4.2. MODEL OF THE FINANCIAL CRISIS
Originally the content of Section 4.2 was described in (Krouglov,
2013).
4.2.1. Introduction
The recent financial crisis has produced a theoretical challenge to
researchers. How could it happen that relatively small disturbances on
financial markets had produced so large consequences in economic life?

138 Alexei Krouglov
I have been thinking about this too. I tried to find a possible answer in
the framework of mathematical dynamics of economic systems
(Krouglov, 2006; 2009). And I assume that I have found a plausible
hypothesis. The very nature of the financial crisis implies a
superposition of the economic forces acting simultaneously on two
connected economic markets – the financial market and real market.
And in some circumstances these fluctuations start amplifying each
other, which is a dynamic process known in mathematics as resonance
phenomenon.
Below I describe a mathematical model of economic system that
consists of two connected markets. The first one is a market of single-
product real economy and the second is a financial market. The
economic forces acting on the markets are expressed through the
system of ordinary differential equations.
I also try to discuss historical parallels related to the financial crises
and examine some practical implications that follow from the proposed
theoretical model.
4.2.2. Model of Interactions between Real Market
and Financial Market
In this section I present some results from the mathematical
dynamics framework of economic systems from (Krouglov, 2006;
2009), which are useful to the development of a model of financial
crisis.
4.2.2.1. Real Product Market
The main assumption is when there are no disturbing economic
forces, market is in an equilibrium position, i.e., the product’s supply
and demand are equal, and they are developing with a constant rate and
the product’s price is fixed.

|     |     |     |     | Models of the Crises  |     |     | 139  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --------------------- | --- | --- | ---- |
When the balance between the product’s supply and demand is
broken, the product market is experiencing an economic force, which
acts to bring the market to a new equilibrium position.
These economic forces are described by the following ordinary

4.2.3. Financial Crisis and Demand Shock
on Financial Market

Here I try to apply the abovementioned mathematical framework to
financial crisis phenomenon.

4.2.3.1. Propagation of Demand Shock on Financial Market
First, below is a qualitative description of the processes we are
interested in on the joint market of real product and money. I assume
| that  a  demand  | on  | the  financial  |     | market  | plunges  (so-called  | demand  |
| ---------------- | --- | --------------- | --- | ------- | -------------------- | ------- |
shock). It causes a supply of the financial product to fall on financial
market. Since the supply of financial product is linked to the demand of
real product in the model, a drop of the supply of financial product
causes the demand for real product to fall. And eventually, the latter
causes a supply of real product to decrease.

Models of the Crises 143
Now, I describe the abovementioned processes mathematically. I
also simplify few equations to eliminate some insignificant details and
make the results more transparent.
At some point in time the equilibrium situation was broken,
and demand on the financial market was reduced by amount ,

4.2.3.2. Historical Developments of Financial Crises
Cause of the Great Depression
Caution, I’m not a professional researcher of the Great Depression.
However, based on a theoretical framework of the financial crisis
described above I see the following economic developments. After the
stock market crash of 1929 there was a weaker demand for the financial
products. Population started withdrawing money from the banks, which
caused many banks to fail. Consecutively, there was a smaller supply of
the financial products (including credit) on the market. Therefore, there
was a weaker demand for the real products on markets. And as a result,
there was a smaller supply of the real products on markets.
Thus, the main cause of the Great Depression was a “hardware
failure” of the banking system to maintain the demand for financial
products on markets manifested itself in the form of bank bankruptcies.
Cause of the Great Recession
The Great Recession was developing in front of our eyes. Here is
how I see its causes. It had been revealed in 2008 that the collateralized
debt obligations, which were a significant part of the financial products,
were previously priced according to the defective formula. That fact
caused a fall in the demand for these financial products, which in fact
caused their prices to plummet. Investment banks were destined to fail
since their assets significantly lost the values. To prevent worse, the US
authorities inserted a large amount of liquidity into the banking system
to prevent it from a complete collapse. Simultaneously, there was a
smaller supply of the financial products (including the credit) on
market. In turn, a smaller supply of the financial products provoked a
weaker demand for the real products on market. Successively, a weaker
demand for the real products caused a smaller supply of the real
products on market.
Hence, the main cause of the Great Recession was a “software
failure” of the banking system to reinstate a demand for the financial

146 Alexei Krouglov
products on markets. However, one can observe a progress compared to
the Great Depression since the world’s economies have managed to
avoid a repeat of the banking system’s “hardware failure” thanks to the
coordinated governmental actions.
4.2.3.3. Practical Reflections on the Great Recession
As I mentioned above, the Federal Reserve System under Ben
Bernanke, in concert with other governmental authorities, helped to
prevent a collapse of the banking system from by inserting the large
amount of liquidity into the latter. In the model’s framework, this
procedure was implemented by increasing the supply of financial
products on market.
However, a continual increase of the supply of financial products
has not brought an expected significant economic improvement for the
markets.
Why hasn’t this happened? The answer can also be found in the
aforesaid model. As can be seen in Eqs. (4.2.21 – 24), the cause of
crisis is a decrease of demand for financial products presented by Eq.
(4.2.21). However, repetitive actions of the Federal Reserve System
were trying to increase a supply of the financial products shown by Eq.
(4.2.22). The excessive supply of financial products orchestrated by the
Federal Reserve System was creating a surplus of the financial products
on financial market, where the surplus has to be absorbed by other
markets such as, for example, commodities and stock markets (where in
turn the surplus was driving the asset prices to artificially high levels
and thereby creating unsubstantiated financial distortions), and was not
solving the origins of the financial crisis.
Additionally, if the hypothesis of a resonance phenomenon
proposed earlier is correct, then in order to even partially overcome the
consequences of the financial crisis it would be required from the
Federal Reserve System to perform continuous monetary interventions

Models of the Crises 147
on a significant scale, and it could possibly create the massive long-
lasting distortions on markets correspondingly.
Figuratively speaking, one can compare the current attempts to
alleviate economic situation with a water tank, which has a pipe
attached to its wall. Water is constantly pouring out of tank through the
pipe. Simultaneously, water is also added to the tank from the top. If
water is pouring in faster than it is pouring out, the tank is being filled.
On the other hand, one could reattach the pipe to the wall at some
higher point thereon perhaps eliminating needs for a continuous water
injection.
It looks to me that the right course of actions for the US and other
governmental authorities would be to work on financial product’s
demand expanding possibilities for the financial markets.
4.2.4. Conclusion
Thus, presented is a simplified mathematical model, which tries to
explain some uncertain aspects that relate to the financial crises. In
particular, the model explains how relatively small disturbances on
financial markets could have produced large consequences in economy.
The model discusses that nature of the financial crisis implies a
superposition of economic forces acting on two connected markets –
the financial market and real market. The fluctuations could amplify
each other, which gives rise to resonance phenomenon. The model
gives us a tool to theoretically evaluate what kind of practical actions
would be most appropriate for the resolution of consequences of the
financial crises.
One might also ask if adding more complexities to the model could
expose other characteristics of the financial crises that are currently
undetected by the simplified mathematical model. The author doesn’t
know the answer to this question.

4.3. MONETARY PART OF ABENOMICS

Originally the content of Section 4.3 was described in (Krouglov,
2014a).

4.3.1. Introduction

| After  | becoming  |     | the  | Prime  Minister  |     | of  Japan,  | Shinzō  | Abe  |
| ------ | --------- | --- | ---- | ---------------- | --- | ----------- | ------- | ---- |
announced an implementation of the economic policy consisting of
three parts (or “arrows”): expanding monetary policy, supportive fiscal
policy, and growth oriented economic reforms. The policy was labeled
by some public intellectuals as Abenomics. The monetary policy part
was implemented in concert with the Bank of Japan.
| The        | Bank        | of  Japan  | started          | buying      | Japanese  | government  |             | bonds.      |
| ---------- | ----------- | ---------- | ---------------- | ----------- | --------- | ----------- | ----------- | ----------- |
| Since the  | demand      | for        | bonds            | increased,  | the       | price       | of  bonds   | initially   |
| increased  | as  well    | and        | correspondingly  |             | the       | bond        | yield       | decreased.  |
| However,   | a  “weird”  |            | outcome          | happened    | later.    | Despite     | continuous  |             |
purchasing of bonds by the Bank of Japan their price significantly
decreased and the yield increased.
This paradoxical event caused some confusion among the economic
experts. One camp was saying the explanation was applicable to the
anticipated recovery of real economy. Particularly, the investors were
anticipating an economic recovery, which would increase demand for
the risky assets and decrease demand for the government bonds, which,
in turn, would project an increase of the bond yield both in the future
and in the present correspondingly. Personally, I found this explanation
| a  bit  convoluted.  |     | The  | other  | camp  was  | saying  | that  | the  | event  was  |
| -------------------- | --- | ---- | ------ | ---------- | ------- | ----- | ---- | ----------- |
associated with financial market’s experience. Primarily, they said the
cautious investors (where the cause of their nervousness was differently
| argued  | by  different  |     | experts)  | were  selling  | the  | Japanese  | government  |     |
| ------- | -------------- | --- | --------- | -------------- | ---- | --------- | ----------- | --- |

|     |     |     | Models of the Crises  |     |     |     |     | 149  |
| --- | --- | --- | --------------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | ---- |
bonds in larger quantities, and overpowering the purchases of them by
the Bank of Japan.
The author considered himself to be in the financial market camp.4
| Nevertheless,  |        | he  decided  | to  build     | a  simplified  |        | model               | to  verify  | his  |
| -------------- | ------ | ------------ | ------------- | -------------- | ------ | ------------------- | ----------- | ---- |
| “intuition”    | where  | the          | model  would  | be             | based  | on  the  framework  |             | of   |
mathematical dynamics of economic systems (Krouglov, 2006; 2009)
developed earlier.
Below the author describes a mathematical model of the financial
| market.        | The     | economic  | forces          | acting  on  | market  | represent  | both          | the  |
| -------------- | ------- | --------- | --------------- | ----------- | ------- | ---------- | ------------- | ---- |
| inherent       | demand  |           | and  supply     | market      | forces  | and        | government    |      |
| interventions  |         | and       | are  expressed  | through     | the     | system     | of  ordinary  |      |
differential equations.
For clarity purposes, the author presents the model in two steps.
First, he simplifies the picture in order to show the underlying impact of
fluctuations. Second, he introduces back the amortization force, which
defines the inflection point of economic system.
| The  | model  | represents  | author’s  | second  | result  | obtained  | from  | the  |
| ---- | ------ | ----------- | --------- | ------- | ------- | --------- | ----- | ---- |
lessons of the Great Recession. The previous result (Krouglov, 2013)
was devoted to the nature of the financial crisis.

4.3.2. Model of Liquidity Pumping into Financial Market

Concepts and methodology presented in this section are based on
| the  framework  |     | of  | mathematical  | dynamics  |     | of  economic  | systems  |     |
| --------------- | --- | --- | ------------- | --------- | --- | ------------- | -------- | --- |
developed in (Krouglov, 2006; 2009).
When there are no disturbing economic forces, financial market is
in equilibrium position, i.e., the supply of and demand for the financial
product are equal, they are developing with a constant rate and the
financial product’s price is fixed.

4 The author had assumed there was an optimum point. If one pumped liquidity less than the
optimum point, the bond prices increased. If one pumped liquidity more than the optimum
point, the bond price decreased.

Thus, in a system where the amortization effect is not present
liquidity pumping into financial market causes the fluctuations of the
financial product’s supply and likewise the financial product’s price on
the market. Therefore, the financial product’s price in such
circumstances experiences a volatile behavior.

.
Thus, we can observe that if liquidity is pumped into the financial
market with a constant rate then the quantitative value of rate
doesn’t have an impact on the financial product’s price volatility.
This fact contradicts what the author predicted earlier (the author had
assumed there was an optimum point: if one pumped liquidity less than
optimum point the bond prices increased; if one pumped liquidity more
than optimum point the bond price decreased).6 Volatility of the

decreases the yield). The price increase swiftly causes a rise of the product’s supply. Yet,

First, loyalty of the Japanese society to government reflected in a
continuous purchase of the Japanese government bonds even when their
yields become miniscule is consistent with a relatively insignificant
| amortization constant  |     |    | 0  for the Japanese economic system.  |     |     |     |     |

the process doesn’t significantly decrease the product’s (market) demand due to a small
amortization constant in Japan. The product’s supply overshoots (i.e., exceeds the product’s
(combined) demand). Hence, the price fluctuates.

It can be seen that Eqs. (4.3.14) and (4.3.21) are identical (though

Consequently, conditions for the fluctuations of solutions of Eqs.
(4.3.14) and (4.3.21) are also the same (i.e., conditions for the financial
product’s price fluctuations are similar if liquidity is pumped into
the financial market with constant rate or if it is pumped with
constant acceleration ). Also, if amortization constant is smaller
than the critical value ( ), the variable fluctuates
and likewise fluctuates the financial product’s price . If
amortization constant is more than the critical value ( ) then
the financial product’s price doesn’t fluctuate.

4.3.5. Conclusion

Presented here is a simplified mathematical model that explains the
effects of liquidity pumping into the financial markets.
There were discussed the implications of liquidity pumping into the
| financial  | markets  | as  | a  part  | of  the  | monetary  | policy  |     | of  so-called  |
| ---------- | -------- | --- | -------- | -------- | --------- | ------- | --- | -------------- |
Abenomics. The author was able to make the following observations
with regard to the monetary policy in question.
First, the model explained the decrease of the Japanese government
| bond  prices  | as  | a  side  | effect  | of  loyalty  | of  | Japanese  | society  | to  the  |
| ------------- | --- | -------- | ------- | ------------ | --- | --------- | -------- | -------- |
government policy in general expressed in the model in the form of
small amortization constant.

7 Not to forget a noted exception of the short-term damped fluctuations when an amortization


| --------------------------------------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| constant is smaller than the critical value ( |     |     |     |     |     | ).  |     |     |
D 
P

160 Alexei Krouglov
Second, pumping the liquidity into the financial markets with
constant rate was able to produce a constrained impact on the prices of
financial products. Eventually this impact would disappear.
Third, amount of the final price adjustment is directly proportional
to the rate of liquidity pumping into the financial market.
Fourth, in order to cause a long lasting impact on the prices of
financial products the monetary policy had to be modified. It would be
required to do the pumping of liquidity into the financial markets not
with a constant rate but with a steadily accelerated rate.
4.4. SECULAR STAGNATION AND DECLINE
Originally the content of Section 4.4 was described in (Krouglov,
2014b).
4.4.1. Introduction
The secular stagnation hypothesis with respect to today’s
economical conditions was put forward by modern economist Lawrence
Summers.8 I found that his ideas were close to my research on
conjectural economic situation, which I had been analyzing using the
models of an economic growth (Krouglov, 2006; 2009). However, his
pragmatic views allowed me to transfer my theoretical constructs from
an imaginary realm of virtual reality into the practical realm of modern-
day economic life.
Previously, I built mathematical model of an economic growth
based on the ordinary differential equations describing demand and
supply forces in economy. The model showed that both constant-rate
8 Reflections of Lawrence Summers on the secular stagnation are available in eBook at
VoxEU.org.

|     |     | Models of the Crises  |     |     |     | 161  |
| --- | --- | --------------------- | --- | --- | --- | ---- |
and constant-acceleration savings and investment did correspondingly
cause a restricted (long-term impact) and an unrestricted (long-term
impact) economic growth. I was examining economic processes on the
market using a hypothetical single-product economy.
Here, I complement that model with a contrasting situation where
the external supply of product comes to market. I show the external
supply provided with either a constant rate or a constant acceleration
causes either a restricted (secular stagnation) or an unrestricted (secular
decline) economic decline.
| Below  | I  describe  | mathematical  | model  | of  an  | economic  | market.  |
| ------ | ------------ | ------------- | ------ | ------- | --------- | -------- |
Economic forces acting on the market represent both inherent market
forces of the demand and supply complemented with the impact of
savings and external supply. The market forces are expressed through
the system of ordinary differential equations.
For clarity, I also describe the situation of an economic growth
| since  contrast  |     | of  economic  | growth  | with  | the  | economic  |
| ---------------- | --- | ------------- | ------- | ----- | ---- | --------- |
stagnation/decline makes obtained conceptual results more practical and
perceptive.

product is fixed.

where , i.e., a rate of the demand in money terms for

I compare the variable , a rate of the demand in money terms
changed by saving and investment on market, with the variable

and .
Thus, if saving and investment on market is done with a
constant acceleration then the value of acceleration causes
an unrestricted increase (decrease) of the rate of demand in money
terms . The rate of demand in money terms continuously
increases (decreases) with the passage of time. It differs from the case
where saving and investment is done with a constant rate , which
produces a converged change of the rate of demand in money terms
constrained by the finite value .

4.4.3.3. Economic Considerations
This section outlines how the process of saving and investment in
single-product economy can create an economic growth in money
terms.
The economic growth in money terms is generated through a dual
impact of changes in the product prices and changes in the supply-
demand equilibrium on the market of product.
After supply-demand equilibrium on the market of product is
reached, the economic growth in money terms is essentially achieved as
a by-product of continuous improvement in the product’s quality. To

| 176      |                 |     | Alexei Krouglov  |           |           |                  |     |
| -------- | --------------- | --- | ---------------- | --------- | --------- | ---------------- | --- |
| improve  | the  product’s  |     | quality          | one  has  | to  make  | an  appropriate  |     |
investment of the product (remember, we are dealing with a single-
product economy here). That is done by applying the product’s savings
and investment, i.e., withdrawing an appropriate amount of product
from the market. That process creates the product’s shortage on the
market, which interrupts supply-demand equilibrium on the market and
drives the product’s price up. In other words, a higher price for the
improved-quality product is caused (from a modeling point of view) by
the necessity to break the supply-demand equilibrium on market via a
partial withdrawal of the product as product’s savings and investment in
order to improve the product’s quality (note, the investment is needed
to improve the product’s quality).
On the other hand, an increase of the product’s price decreases the
demand for product. Depending on particular characteristics of product
saving and investment, the dual effect of price increase and demand
decrease can cause either an economic growth in money terms or an
economic decline in money terms.
Additionally, a removal of the product from market in the form of
savings and investment decreases the available amount of product. The
reduced amount of product on market is compensated by increase of the
| product’s     | supply.          | Thus,       | simultaneous    | removal        | of         | the  product        | from  |
| ------------- | ---------------- | ----------- | --------------- | -------------- | ---------- | ------------------- | ----- |
| market        | for  investment  |             | and  increment  | of  the        | product’s  | supply              | have  |
| opposite      | impacts          | on          | the  market     | with  regard   | to         | the  supply-demand  |       |
| equilibrium.  | As               | a  result,  | the             | work  of  two  | opposite   | forces              | may   |
demonstrate itself as either a product surplus or a product shortage on
the market at some point in time but the market will try to bring itself to
new supply-demand equilibrium in the long run.
Moreover, the observations can be made about characteristics of
product’s savings and investment and their impact on an economic
growth in money terms or an economic decline in money terms. If
| removal  | of  the  | product  | from  | market  in  | the  form  | of  savings  | and  |
| -------- | -------- | -------- | ----- | ----------- | ---------- | ------------ | ---- |
investment is performed with a constant rate the effect is a restricted
long-term economic growth in money terms. If removal of the product

|     |     | Models of the Crises  |     |     |     | 177  |
| --- | --- | --------------------- | --- | --- | --- | ---- |
from market in the form of savings and investment is done with a
constant acceleration of modest value the effect is an unrestricted long-
term economic growth in money terms. If removal of the product from
market in the form of savings and investment is done with a constant
| acceleration  | of  | large  value  | the  | effect  is  an  | unrestricted  | long-term  |
| ------------- | --- | ------------- | ---- | --------------- | ------------- | ---------- |
economic decline in money terms.

4.4.4. Model of Economic Decline in Money Terms

An economic decline studied here is fundamentally driven by the
external supply of product.
I present model where a constant-rate or a constant-acceleration
| external  | supply  | are  causing  | either  | restricted  | economic  | decline  or  |
| --------- | ------- | ------------- | ------- | ----------- | --------- | ------------ |
unrestricted economic decline correspondingly.
