I will talk about some economic implications of results in the next
section.

4.4.4.3. Economic Considerations
This section outlines how external supply of product in a single-
product economy can create an economic decline in money terms.
The economic decline in money terms is generated through a dual
impact of the changes of product prices and changes in supply-demand
equilibrium on market of product.
The economic decline in money terms described here is caused by
the oversupply of product that violates previously achieved supply-
demand equilibrium on market of product. To capitalize on market of
product an external producer supplies its production to the market. The
external supply of product creates a product surplus on market, which
| interrupts  | supply-demand  |     | equilibrium  |     |     | on  market  | and  | drives  the  |
| ----------- | -------------- | --- | ------------ | --- | --- | ----------- | ---- | ------------ |
product’s price down. In other words, a lower price of the product on
market is caused by the interruption of supply-demand equilibrium via
extra supply of product from an external producer.
On the other hand, a decrease of the product’s price increases the
| product’s  | demand.  | Depending  |     | on  | particular  |     | characteristics  | of  an  |
| ---------- | -------- | ---------- | --- | --- | ----------- | --- | ---------------- | ------- |

Models of the Crises 191
external supply of product, the dual effect of price decrease and demand
increase can cause either an economic growth in money terms or an
economic decline in money terms.
Additionally, an external supply of product on market increases the
available amount of product. The increased amount of product on
market is compensated by decrease of product supply from an internal
producer. Thus, simultaneous provision of the extra supply of product
on market from an external producer and reduction of the product
supply from an internal producer has opposite impacts on the market
with regard to supply-demand equilibrium. As a result, the work of two
opposite forces may demonstrate itself as either the product’s surplus or
product’s shortage on market at some points in time but the market will
try to bring itself to a new supply-demand equilibrium position in the
long run.
Moreover, observations can be made about characteristics of the
external supply of product and their impact on an economic growth in
money terms or an economic decline in money terms. If external supply
of product on market comes with a constant rate the effect is a restricted
long-term economic decline in money terms. Such restricted economic
decline in money terms corresponds to the secular stagnation. If
external supply of product on market comes with a constant
acceleration the effect is an unrestricted long-term economic decline in
money terms. Such unrestricted economic decline in money terms
corresponds to the secular decline.
4.4.5. Economic Implications
Thus, an economic growth in money terms (conceptually described
for a single-product economy here) is fundamentally caused by the
processes of savings and investment. An investment of product is used
to improve the quality of product itself. Savings of product causes a

192 Alexei Krouglov
decrease of the quantity of available product on market, which causes
an increase of the product’s price. And increase of the product’s price
causes a decrease of the demand for product. A dual effect of the price
increase and demand decrease can cause either an economic growth in
money terms or an economic decline in money terms.
With regard to an economic growth/decline in money terms caused
by the product’s investment there are considered three distinct
scenarios. If investment of product is performed with a constant rate the
result is shown as a limited long-term economic growth in money
terms. If investment of product is done with a modest constant
acceleration the result is revealed as an unlimited long-term economic
growth in money terms. If investment of product is achieved with a
large constant acceleration the result is demonstrated as an unlimited
long-term economic decline in money terms.12
On the other hand, an economic decline in money terms
(conceptually shown for a single-product economy) is fundamentally
caused by the external supply of product to market. The external supply
of product violates supply-demand equilibrium on market and creates
the product’s surplus, which drives the product’s price lower. In other
words, a decline of the product’s price on market is caused by
interruption of supply-demand equilibrium from the extra supply of
product by external producer. And decrease of the product’s price
causes an increase of the demand for product. Dual effect of the price
decrease and demand increase can cause either an economic growth in
money terms or an economic decline in money terms.
The extra supply of product on market by an external producer is
compensated by decrease of the product’s supply from an internal
producer. Thus, extra supply of product to market from an external
producer and reduction of the product’s supply from an internal
producer has opposite impacts on market’s supply-demand equilibrium.
12 I am ignoring effects of both international trade and technological advances on the economic
growth in money terms.

Models of the Crises 193
The resultant work of two opposite factors may demonstrate itself either
as the product’s surplus or as the product’s shortage on market at some
points in time but the market forces will try to bring market to a new
supply-demand equilibrium position in the long run.
With regard to an economic growth/decline in money terms caused
by external supply of product there are identified two distinct scenarios.
If external supply of product on market comes with a constant rate the
effect is a limited long-term economic decline in money terms
conventionally called a secular stagnation. If external supply of product
on market comes with a constant acceleration the effect is an unlimited
long-term economic decline in money terms. Such unlimited economic
decline in money terms may be called a secular decline.
Thus, economic policies related to handling of the secular
stagnation according to the presented model can be summarized as
follows.
First, policy makers should prevent a transformation of secular
stagnation into the secular decline. It means the markets should be
protected from supply of products from external suppliers brought with
a persistently accelerated rate, which would case an unlimited long-
term economic decline in money terms. The protection measures can be
either bureaucratic obstacles or economic tariffs and quotas etc.
Second, impact of secular stagnation per se can be handled via a
two-stage policy. The first stage is to counterbalance an impact on
markets of external supply of products from external suppliers brought
with constant rate, which would cause a limited long-term economic
decline in money terms (a.k.a. the secular stagnation). Alleviations can
be done by addressing the origin of the problem, namely, a violation of
the supply-demand equilibrium on markets by external supply of
products. Actually, the best way to equalize the external supply of
products on markets is to advance the additional demand for products.
However, the additional demand for products would still alleviate a
limited long-term economic decline in money terms exhibited in the
form of secular stagnation but it would not be able to cause an

194 Alexei Krouglov
economic growth in money terms itself. The latter can be ensured by
application of the second stage of economic policy. Thus, the second
stage is to commence processes of savings and investment in order to
stipulate an economic growth in money terms. The savings and
investment should be done with a modest acceleration, which would
result in an unlimited long-term economic growth in money terms. If
the savings and investment are achieved with a large acceleration, it
would result in an unlimited long-term economic decline in money
terms. If the savings and investments are performed with a constant rate
it would result in a limited long-term economic growth in money terms.
It is worth to note that the aforesaid two-stage policies can be executed
concurrently.
4.4.6. Conclusion
Presented here is a simplified mathematical model that explains the
effects of an economic growth and economic decline in money terms.
Among the considered cases are a limited long-term economic growth
in money terms, an unlimited long-term economic growth in money
terms, a limited long-term economic decline in money terms (or secular
stagnation), and an unlimited long-term economic decline in money
terms (or secular decline).
Thus, the economic policies related to handling of the secular
stagnation, which follow from presented model, can be summarized as
below.
First, the measures should be taken to prevent transformation of the
secular stagnation into the secular decline.
Second, the impact of secular stagnation per se should be handled
via a two-stage policy. The first stage is to counterbalance the impact
on markets of external supply of products from external suppliers via a
policy of advancing the additional demand for products. The additional

Models of the Crises 195
demand would alleviate a limited long-term nominal economic decline
exhibited in the form of secular stagnation. Besides, the second stage of
economic policy is to boost the processes of savings and investment in
order to stipulate an economic growth in money terms. The savings and
investments should be done with a modest acceleration, which would
result in an unlimited long-term economic growth in money terms. Both
stages of economic policies can be executed concurrently.
4.5. MODEL OF THE GREEK CRISIS
Originally the content of Section 4.5 was described in (Krouglov,
2015c).
4.5.1. Introduction
Greek economy has been in a dire economic recession. Few factors
have brought Greece into the crisis and are preventing the recovery.
They include (i) debt burden and corresponding necessity to service the
debt, (ii) the lack of investment that induce an economic growth, and
(iii) import of (cheaper and better quality) consumer goods from abroad
that suffocate local manufacturing.
Here I present a simplified mathematical model describing four
consecutive stages of the Greek crisis (before the Eurozone, before the
Euro crisis, after the Euro crisis, and during the austerity period) and
two possible recovery phases (corresponding to an austere economic
transformation and a benign economic transformation).
I build a mathematical model of the market of single-product
economy, which can produce an explicit description of the interaction
among relevant economic variables. Economic forces that act on market
represent inherent market forces of the demand and supply

196 Alexei Krouglov
complemented with forces caused by the investment, external supply of
product on market, debt and debt servicing. The market forces are
expressed through the system of ordinary differential equations.
The work uses results for an economic growth and decline in money
terms obtained in (Krouglov, 2014b), results for a credit expansion and
contraction received in (Krouglov, 2015a), and results for impact of the
debt and investment on an economic growth obtained in (Krouglov,
2015b).
4.5.2. Mathematical Model of the Greek Crisis
Concepts and methodology presented in this paper are based on the
framework of mathematical dynamics of economic systems developed
in (Krouglov, 2006; 2009). When there are no disturbing economic
forces, market is in equilibrium position, i.e., the supply of and demand
for product are equal and are developing with a constant rate and the
price of product is fixed. When balance between the volumes of
product’s supply and demand is broken, market is experiencing
economic forces, which act to bring the market to a new equilibrium
position.
The framework was extended with new results. Conditions for an
economic growth and decline in money terms were investigated in
(Krouglov, 2014b). Questions of a credit expansion and contraction
with regard to the economic growth were investigated in (Krouglov,
2015a). A mutual impact of the debt and investment on economic
growth was explored in (Krouglov, 2015b).
These theoretical results will be used to develop a simplified model
of the Greek crisis.

4.5.2.3. Model of Greek Economy after the Euro Crisis

0
) an interest
rate for the Greek debt dramatically increased. Greece couldn’t borrow

problem for Greek economy. At the same time Greece couldn’t use the
debt anymore as a tool to increase the demand. It couldn’t also pay for
the imported consumer goods.

We can see the Greek economy was in a state of free fall after the
Euro crisis. The accelerated pace of debt servicing cost would cause a
continuous price fall (a.k.a. deflation). The rate and the amount of debt
| servicing  cost  | could  | eventually  |     | exceed  | the  rate  | and  | the  | volume  of  |
| ---------------- | ------ | ----------- | --- | ------- | ---------- | ---- | ---- | ----------- |
demand for product. The rate of demand in money terms would be
| steadily  decreasing  |     | with  | a  constant  |     | deceleration.  |     | Clearly,  | such  |
| --------------------- | --- | ----- | ------------ | --- | -------------- | --- | --------- | ----- |
economic situation could not last for a long time.

Thus, we can see the austerity measures and import of consumer
goods from other countries brought Greek economy to the situation
worse than prior of joining the Eurozone. This economic situation could
last for a long time if only Greek population keeps its patience.
Note that external supply of product caused the prices to fall (i.e.,
created deflation). When external supply of product is provided with a

206 Alexei Krouglov
constant rate, the effect is a restricted long-term economic decline in
money terms (a.k.a. secular stagnation), which was described in
(Krouglov, 2014b).
4.5.3. Model of Economic Transformations
I present in this section discussion what might constitute a recovery
for the Greek economy.
According to (Krouglov, 2014b), an economic growth in money
terms is fundamentally driven (among other factors) by the continuous
flow of investment.
If investment of product is done with a constant rate the result is a
limited long-term economic growth in money terms. If investment of
product is done with a modest constant acceleration the result is an
unlimited long-term economic growth in money terms. If investment of
product is done with a large constant acceleration the result is an
unlimited long-term economic decline in money terms.
Let me show how a constant-rate investment of product can benefit
the Greek economy. I explore two options: the first one is where the
demand is cut due to the austerity measures; and the second one is
where the demand is sustained to alleviate economic hardship.

(Krouglov, 2014b). Eurozone countries promised to help Greece with
handling its debt problem. However, they don’t allow Greece to use the
debt to increase the demand and want Greece to continue importing
consumer goods from them.

, i.e., a rate of the expanded demand in money terms increases relatively
to the rates before both joining the Eurozone and the Euro crisis. The
economic transformation brings here good results. The economic
situation could last for a long time.
4.5.3.3. Discussion and Interpretation
We can briefly compare results of the pro-growth transformation of
Greek economy obtained for both austere and benign economic
conditions. It can be seen that austere conditions create a big hurdle in
the form of the rate of external supply of products. In order to have an
economic growth, Greek economy has to use investment with a rate
exceeding the rate of external supply of products. On the other hand,
during benign economic conditions when the rate of external supply of
products is balanced through increase of a demand the hurdle is

|     | Models of the Crises  |     |     |     | 213  |
| --- | --------------------- | --- | --- | --- | ---- |
nonexistent. Even investment with a small rate14 can produce economic
| growth,  which  | would  be  | helpful  for  | the  Greek  | economy  | that  |
| --------------- | ---------- | ------------- | ----------- | -------- | ----- |
experiences a deep economic recession.

4.5.4. Conclusion

| A  simplified  | economic  | model  describing  |     | the  current  | Greek  |
| -------------- | --------- | ------------------ | --- | ------------- | ------ |
economic crisis is presented.
The theoretical result is that study of different stages of the crisis
was done with a mathematical accuracy.
As practical result, it was objectively shown that investment in
| benign  conditions  | of  the  | sustained  demand  | would  | produce a  | better  |
| ------------------- | -------- | ------------------ | ------ | ---------- | ------- |
economic growth in money terms than investment in austere conditions
of the reduced demand.

14 Of course, investment with an accelerated rate would produce better result as seen in
(Krouglov, 2014b).

SUMMARY
The main goal of this book is to present coherent mathematical
models to describe an economic growth and related economic issues.
The book is a continuation of author’s previous book (Krouglov, 2006)
where there were presented mathematical models of economic forces
acting on the markets. In (Krouglov, 2006) the author described a
system of ordinary differential equations, which connected together
economic forces behind the product’s demand, supply and prices on the
market.
In this book the author focused on a specific aspect how to modify
the said system of ordinary differential equations in order to describe
the phenomenon of an economic growth. In order to achieve clarity, the
author restricted himself to economic processes arising on the markets
of a single-product economy. His conclusion was that economic growth
is a result of savings and investment occurring on the markets. The
explanation is that markets participants withdraw part of the product
from markets in the form of savings and use the withdrawn product in
production in the form of investment. The withdrawal drives the
product’s supply on market down while at the same time it drives the
product’s price on markets up, which in turn drives the product’s
demand down. When an impact of the product’s price increase exceeds

| 216  |     |     |     | Alexei Krouglov  |     |     |     |     |     |
| ---- | --- | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
an impact of the product’s demand decrease we observe an economic
| growth  | whereas  |     | we  observe  | an  | economic  | decline  | in  | the  | opposite  |
| ------- | -------- | --- | ------------ | --- | --------- | -------- | --- | ---- | --------- |
situation.
| Then        | the             | author  | looked     | into    | various  | aspects         | that  | savings   | and       |
| ----------- | --------------- | ------- | ---------- | ------- | -------- | --------------- | ----- | --------- | --------- |
| investment  | exerted         |         | on  the    | market  | of       | single-product  |       | economy.  | The       |
| author      | in  particular  |         | discussed  | the     | models   | examining       |       | an        | economic  |
growth in situations when savings and investment was done in the form
of one-time withdrawal of product, constant-rate withdrawal of product,
constant-accelerated withdrawal of product, and exponential withdrawal
of product from the market.
The author further examined an impact of four important economic
concepts on an economic growth – demand, supply, investment, and
| debt.  He  | presented  |     | mathematical  |     | models  | exploring  | interconnections  |     |     |
| ---------- | ---------- | --- | ------------- | --- | ------- | ---------- | ----------------- | --- | --- |
among these concepts and studied their mutual impacts on both an
economic growth and decline. He also built mathematical model in
order to verify a hypothesis that weak recovery after the financial crisis
| could  | be  attributed  |     | to  the  | decline  | of  | investments  |     | that  | were  not  |
| ------ | --------------- | --- | -------- | -------- | --- | ------------ | --- | ----- | ---------- |
compensated by the decrease of an interest rate.
The author also looked into the phenomenon of economic crises and
built few mathematical models for this purpose. The models of four
economic crises were considered. The first model was about the last
| financial  | crisis  | where  | author  | tried  | to  | explain  | how  relatively  |     | small  |
| ---------- | ------- | ------ | ------- | ------ | --- | -------- | ---------------- | --- | ------ |
disturbances on financial markets had produced a large impact on real
economy. His conclusion was that fluctuations on connected markets
were amplifying each other, which is known as resonance phenomenon.
| The  second  |     | model  | was  | related  | to  the  | monetary  | part  | of  | Japanese  |
| ------------ | --- | ------ | ---- | -------- | -------- | --------- | ----- | --- | --------- |
economic policy known as Abenomics. Despite purchasing of the bonds
by the Bank of Japan their price decreased and the yield increased. The
| author                                        | constructed  |     | a  mathematical  |     |           | model     | to  investigate     |      | this    |
| --------------------------------------------- | ------------ | --- | ---------------- | --- | --------- | --------- | ------------------- | ---- | ------- |
| phenomenon. The third model was devoted to a  |              |     |                  |     |           |           | secular stagnation  |      |         |
| hypothesis                                    | advanced     |     | by  economist    |     | Lawrence  | Summers.  |                     | The  | author  |
complemented a model of economic growth with the external supply of
product to market. The results were that external supply provided with

Summary 217
either constant rate or constant acceleration could cause a restricted or
unrestricted economic decline respectively. The fourth model was a
model describing four stages of the Greek economic crisis (before the
Eurozone, before the Euro crisis, after the Euro crisis, and during the
austerity period) and two potential recovery stages (with austere and
benign economic transformations).
Therefore, the book continues a long-standing tradition of
presenting mathematical models to discuss the phenomena of economic
processes on markets.
